(3) *Nesting warning.* Seatbelt cannot nest on macOS (`sandbox-exec: sandbox_apply: Operation not permitted`), so a sandboxed Codex lead cannot start sandboxed `codex exec` workers. `batch-plan` therefore writes one WARNING line into SUMMARY.md (bound by the approval hash) when a Codex session can run as the lead (`runtime.lead`, `batch.runtimes`, or the installed fallback runtime tried when the first hits its usage limit; the same `rt_list` the driver uses), the plan was made without `--codex-danger-full-access` and at least one role resolves to codex; it names the roles and the two ways out: claude workers and reviewers (`proofline init --worker claude --reviewers claude`, no `--role ROLE=codex`), or planning again with `--codex-danger-full-access`. It is a warning, not a block. (4) *Session records.* Each session writes `.agents/local/batch/sessions/<ID>.<n>.rec` (key=value: id, n, runtime, method, log, start, then end, exit, timeout); `batch status` has a sessions section (task, #, runtime, start, end, exit, park reason, log; `124 (timeout)`; `no end recorded`), so a first real run can be diagnosed from records; a state without `sessions/` prints no section; `batch-plan` moves `sessions` into history with the other files. (5) *Markdown-only adapter, by design.* `batch-plan` refuses a non-markdown `tasks.adapter` (exit 1, nothing written) with the next step: set `tasks.adapter` to markdown, import each task with `/proofline task <issue reference>`, run `/proofline batch <ID...>` again. Reasons: `depends_on` and `Suggested class` live in the task file's own front matter and sections, and github, jira and linear resolve ids through network calls that an unattended driver must not depend on at night. (6) *Kill guard (#127).* The `proofline-hook.sh` scanner emits two more record kinds, the run path of a literal command word and here-document bodies; it skips `#` comments and `case` labels, treats `$((...))` as one word, ends `< <(cmd)` correctly, and strips leading tabs from the delimiter only for `<<-`. `kill_guard` now also denies: PID operands `0` or `-1` in any spelling (`00`, `+0`, `$((0))`); PIDs from `pgrep`, `pidof`, `ps`, `lsof`, `fuser`, `netstat`, `ss`, `pstree` or `jps` that reach `kill` through a variable, a `for` or `read` loop, `mapfile` or `$@` in the same command; `find -exec kill|pkill` and `find | xargs kill`; `eval` and a variable command word whose value the same command assigns; `setsid`, `su -c`, `flock` and similar wrappers; interpreter one-liners, here-documents and piped text (`python -c`, `perl -e`, `node -e`, `ruby -e`, `php -r`, `lua -e`, `awk` (only where it can run a command: `system(`, a pipe to or from a command, a coprocess), `osascript -e`) containing `pkill`, `killall`, `slay`, `fuser -k`, `os.kill`/`process.kill`/`Process.kill` with 0 or -1, `killpg(getpgrp())`, psutil loops that kill, or string literals that are such command lines; the unquoted here-document body is scanned for `$(...)` and backticks because the shell executes them; and the content of script files the command runs (`sh|bash x`, `./x`, `source x`, `python|node|perl|ruby x`) or writes in the same command, when the file is a text file of at most 256 KB under the project (not `node_modules` or `.git`) or a temp dir. `kill -TERM "-$pgid"` is no longer mistaken for `xargs kill`. Decision, tripwire not boundary: the guard stops an agent's honest mistake (killing a user's processes by name); it is not a security boundary against a hostile agent, whose boundary is the runtime's permission mode and sandbox. What stays unseen, and why each is decided: a payload assembled at run time from data the hook cannot see (`$(curl ...)`, a decoded string, a variable assigned in an earlier tool call), because nothing is there to read and a rule over arbitrary `$VAR` or `eval "$x"` would deny `eval "$(brew shellenv)"`; a script another tool call writes and a later call runs from outside the project and temp dirs, a file over 256 KB, a binary, a symlink, and `node_modules` code, because third-party code legitimately contains `kill` text and reading arbitrary paths would mean scanning system files; a PID taken from a search in an earlier command (`pgrep x > f`, then `kill $(cat f)`), because `kill $(cat pidfile)` is the normal way to stop what the agent started; a variable command word assigned in an earlier call; `kill -- -$PGID` and `process.kill(-child.pid)`, which stay allowed because killing a group by number is how an agent stops the tree of its own detached child (only literal 0 and -1 and a group from `getpgrp()` are denied); and interpreter code that only prints these words, which is denied because text and an exec target cannot be told apart; the denial text now says exactly that and that the user can run the command with the `!` prefix. Also unseen, each with its reason: another tool's text (`npm run`, `make` and package.json scripts), because the commands inside are text that the hook never sees and resolving them means running or parsing that tool; remote or container targets (`ssh host pkill x`, `docker exec c pkill x`), because they signal processes on another machine or in a container, not the user's local processes, denying them would stop legitimate remote administration, and the user's own machine is what the rule protects; the part of a single here-document body beyond 200000 characters, which the scanner truncates and treats as unreadable; and a relative script path that cannot be resolved (`cd` to a non-literal directory, or a script calling another by a path relative to its own directory), while `cd dir &&` with a literal directory is resolved. Env records: a `NAME=` argument is not an environment assignment, only a prefix before the command word is, so `ps -o pid=` no longer breaks the pending-substitution and variable taint logic. Also denied: `xargs pkill`, taint through arrays and `< <(pgrep x)` read loops, `cat x.sh | sh`, and scripts after `cd dir &&`; long shell options (`--norc`) are parsed correctly. Interpreter rules are restricted to kill-by-name verbs, pid 0 and -1, `getpgrp` group kills, psutil `process_iter` loops that kill, code that kills what `pgrep`, `pidof` or `lsof` returned, and `os.system` or exec of those verbs; a kill of a pid the code spawned itself (`child.pid`, a Popen handle) is not a kill fed by a search, and code that reads `/proc` or runs `ps -o rss= -p <pid>` is allowed. Cost: script text is read only when it holds `kill`, `slay` or `fuser` (or a variable command word). The guard has its own 20 s budget: past it, a command or text that holds a kill word is denied and everything else is allowed, because a command that cannot be read in time must not pass a kill. Before, the hook's 30 s timeout let the command through untouched, fail-open, per Claude Code's documented non-blocking hook timeout. The largest repo script (157 KB) measured 47.7 s before and 4.4 s after. (7) *Freshness and fingerprint (#128, #131, #132, #133).* #128: `unsupported_evidence_check` accepts a validator `BASE:` that is the run's base or a base before an accepted control-plane-only move, resolved through the run's `base-moved` ledger lines (`base_moved_predecessors`, at most 100 links); an unrelated base is still refused, and migration evidence has no `BASE:` line. #132: `control_plane_only` treats an AGENTS.md or CLAUDE.md that HEAD does not have yet (untracked or newly added) as control plane when the whole working-tree file is the proofline block, so `reopen`, `verify-scope` and the tier agree with the fingerprint; extra text keeps it a product change, and a file that exists in HEAD keeps the A154/A155 outside-the-block comparison. #133: `content_fingerprint legacy` computes the 0.0.12/0.0.13 value, and `gate_focus_status` accepts a gate record equal to it only when a block-only AGENTS.md or CLAUDE.md exists; the two rules differ only in such a file, so every product byte stays bound, nothing is written, and records written since 0.0.14 never take this path. #131, design decision: a new AGENTS.md or CLAUDE.md with project text besides the block, a symlink, or a file without markers stays a product change, because it is the instructions every agent reads and accepting appended text would let a control-plane commit carry project rules past the plan and the scope check; the stale-plan message and the scope check now name the file (`Changed outside the control plane: AGENTS.md (...)`) and the action (amend and refreeze, or move the text out and keep only the block). (8) *SEO (#137, #140).* robots.txt rule paths and page paths are compared in the same RFC 9309 percent-encoded normal form (`Disallow: /caf%C3%A9/` blocks `café/index.html`, `/a%20b` blocks `a b`, `%7e` equals `~`, hex case ignored, a lone `%` becomes `%25`). A page file `dir/index.html` is tested under the URL form it is published under: its sitemap `<loc>`, else its canonical, else both forms, and in the last case it counts as blocked only when both are blocked, so `Disallow: /dir/index.html` no longer fails a page published as `/dir/`. `siteRel` and `pageUrl` decode with a safe decoder (valid escapes decoded, a malformed `%` stays as written; `decodeURIComponent` threw), except `%2F`, `%25`, `%3F` and `%23`, which stay encoded, so `/a%2Fb` is not read as `/a/b` and `Disallow: /a/b` does not block it. (9) *Real Chrome.* `tests/skills/visual-proof-seo-browser.test.mjs` runs the real `measure.mjs` in Chrome against a local http server (the "third party" is the same server under the host name `localhost`; no network) and skips when `measure.mjs --detect` finds no browser. Observed: an inert `type="text/plain"` script is not requested and gives no consent item; an executable third-party script before consent fails; a first-party loader that calls `fetch` and adds a third-party script is attributed by its initiator; a parser-initiated third-party stylesheet under a `stats` path is not analytics; with `seo.site_url` set to the test origin nothing is third party. (10) *Missing sitemap.xml or robots.txt is information (`not_measured`), by design,* for three reasons: RFC 9309 defines a missing robots.txt as "everything may be crawled", so its absence is a valid state; a sitemap is optional; and with a `file:` or localhost base the measured folder is not the deployed host, so the deployed files cannot be proven absent and a blocking finding would have no evidence. A blocking finding was rejected (it would fail projects that deploy these files at build time) and a warning severity was rejected (the report has only pass, fail and not_measured). (11) *Image initiator, by design.* Chrome's DevTools protocol reports an `<img>` load made at parser time as `{type: parser, url: <document>}` and one made from a timer as `{type: other}`; neither names a script, and `Network.setAttachDebugStack` changes nothing. `fetch`, `sendBeacon` and dynamically added scripts do report the script. A tracking pixel is therefore attributed by its own URL and the project's candidate list only. Patching the page (wrapping `Image`) to learn the caller would alter the page being measured, which the measurement must not do, so the page is not patched. (12) *Only Googlebot and Bingbot are checked, by decision:* they are the bots the spec names; any other token would run through the same code path and is added by extending the list. Prefix tokens such as `googlebot-news` are different tokens under RFC 9309 (a group matches by the bot's own product token), so a `googlebot-news` group is not applied to Googlebot. Why: a night batch could leave a hung session's children running when no group method existed; Codex sessions could not commit or write run records under the legacy sandbox mode and had no network; the kill guard let an agent kill by name through variables, loops, interpreters and scripts; a valid run was refused after a base move, an uncommitted block-only AGENTS.md blocked `reopen`, gates recorded under the 0.0.12/0.0.13 rule read stale after an upgrade; robots rules written in percent-encoded form or against `index.html` were mismatched. Alternatives rejected: a pid-only mode when no group method works (a hung session's children would outlive it); committing in the driver instead of the Codex session (a large redesign that changes who commits); keeping legacy `workspace-write` (cannot write `.git`); denying every `kill $VAR` (an agent's own PID is normal) and every kill-like word in shell scripts (a shell script is parsed as commands, only interpreter code gets the text rules); negative literal PIDs other than `-1` (a group the code started itself); reading arbitrary paths in the hook; rewriting stored gate records (sealed); writing a ledger line from a read-only command; accepting any base on evidence; accepting two fingerprints without the block-only precondition; treating text beside the block as control plane; patching the page to find an image's initiator; a blocking or warning severity for a missing sitemap or robots.txt. Limits, each decided: `git push -u` pushes but cannot record the upstream because `.git/config` is read-only (the delivery step checks `refs/remotes`, so `push -u` is unchanged); a worktree outside the project and `/tmp` itself are not writable in the profile (proofline worktrees live under the project and `$TMPDIR` is writable); a Codex lead on macOS cannot dispatch sandboxed Codex workers because sandboxes cannot nest, and the choice between claude workers and `--codex-danger-full-access` stays with the user, surfaced by the WARNING, which also covers the installed fallback runtime; no real Codex model session ran, only `codex sandbox` and `codex exec` up to the credential check, so the first real run is the user's and a problem opens a new issue; an approval (commit, push, summary) bound to an old fingerprint is asked once more after such an upgrade; package installs in the Codex night profile are verified only for npm, pnpm, yarn, pip and go; the kill-guard unseen forms in (6).
- **A162. 0.0.20, shortcut comments and a README bound, a process-group timeout for command discovery, a `logging` focus, entry-point validation by design (amends A153, A161; related A78).** What: (1) *Shortcut comments.* Five comments carried a foreign shortcut label; they now read `shortcut: <limit>, <when to improve>` so `proofline debt` lists them. `tests/shared/check-static.sh` fails on a foreign label (the allowed tag list is one variable) and on a README above a bound in lines and in bytes; CI's static job and the self-tests run it. (2) *Discovery timeout.* `run_with_timeout` in `bin/lib/discover.sh` starts its command as a process-group leader with the same four methods as the night-mode driver (`set -m`, `setsid`, perl, python3), accepts a method only when a probe reports pgid equal to pid, and on timeout closes the group with TERM then KILL, never pgid 0, 1 or its own group, so a hung `sh -c` leaves no grandchildren. `nap`, `grp_alive` and `pg_start` are a copy of the driver's functions, kept identical by a drift test. When no method works the command is killed directly, documented as a shortcut comment. (3) *README.* Slimmed; night-mode and process-rule detail moved to `docs/concepts/night-mode.md`. (4) *Logging.* A deterministic `logging` focus opens in SECURITY when a line the diff adds is a log call (config `risk.log_sensitive.content`) and names a sensitive word (`risk.log_sensitive.words`: password, token, secret, authorization, cookie, email, phone, card, iban, ssn, tckn; both extensible), matched per added line, never per file. The focus only opens a review: the exploit-proof skill gets a `[log]` sink (request bodies, Authorization and Cookie headers, tokens, passwords, secret keys, personal data, CR/LF injection into plain-text logs) and a finding still needs a concrete path from a source to the log call. The implementer role uses the project's own logger and the standard written in AGENTS.md, and the AGENTS.md template carries no logging text (it is the whole hash-tracked proofline block); the project's own "Logging" section lives outside the block, where `upgrade` leaves it alone, and the `rules` focus checks it. The log-call pattern covers Console, Debug and Trace calls, `Fprint*` in any case, paren-less `print` and `puts`, `var_dump` and `dump`, so `json.dump(password)` also opens a review (the focus only opens a review and yields no finding); `config-check` refuses an invalid `risk.log_sensitive.content` expression and an empty or missing `words`, which silently fell back to a whole-file match. (5) *Entry-point validation, two tiers.* A `## Contracts` bullet of PLAN.md is a mandatory entry point only when it declares one (method plus path, endpoint, webhook, queue consumer, graphql mutation, rpc method, CLI flag or command, `validation.entry_words`), is not that entry point's own removal, is not marked unchanged or documented (`entry_skip`), and marks new or changed input (an `entry_input` word, or an `entry_new` word with no `entry_output` word). Such an entry point must carry one `## Validation` line: `accepts <what>; limits <limits>; rejected at <where, server side>`. `agent.sh freeze` and `refreeze` refuse a plan with a mandatory entry point and no complete line, and refuse a QA plan without an `[invalid-input]` scenario naming it; the plan-contract and qa-plan validators apply the same rules. Output-only and unchanged bullets, bare noun mentions and removals are excluded; a bullet that only mentions an entry word (`entry_candidates`) is information: `agent.sh pipeline` lists it as `validation.possible` and the freeze prints a note, nothing refuses. `agent.sh entry-tier "<bullet>"` prints `mandatory`, `information` or `none`, and the corpus `tests/fixtures/entry-points/corpus.tsv` (run by `tests/fixtures/entry-points/run-corpus.sh`, which prints precision and recall) pins the tiers. The expressions are the `validation.*` keys (`entry_words`, `entry_candidates`, `entry_input`, `entry_new`, `entry_output`, `entry_skip`, `code_patterns`); both validators carry the same defaults and read the project's `validation.*` keys themselves, and `config-check` validates the block. `validation.*` is read at freeze and refreeze and is not hash-bound; a refreeze of a run frozen before the rule re-validates the plan, so the amendment needs the `## Validation` line and the `[invalid-input]` scenario. Code patterns (`validation.code_patterns`) over scope files and the diff are information only. (6) *Legacy risk freeze.* The upgrade adds the default signal `risk.log_sensitive`, which `risk_sha256` binds. A freeze whose hash equals the hash of the block without that key stays valid, and that run keeps its frozen focus set with no `logging` focus; a run frozen under the new rule hashes the key, so editing it is refused. `proofline upgrade` therefore does not invalidate the freeze of runs in flight. (7) *Static rule.* The foreign-shortcut-label rule reads the whole comment part of a line (a leading `'` counts as a comment prefix only at line start, to avoid strings); a prose line that starts with `ponytail:` would also fail. Why: the foreign label was invisible to `proofline debt`; the old timeout killed only the direct child; a README that carries every detail is read by nobody; sensitive data in logs is a common leak and a word match on added lines is cheap and needs no model; validation of external input was left to reviewer attention and nothing asked the plan or the QA plan to say where input is rejected, and the one deterministic source before the freeze is PLAN.md; a single broad word list made ordinary contract bullets (`consumers: billing`, `The handler returns 404`) refuse the freeze and any removal word anywhere exempted a real entry point, hence two tiers; re-binding the risk hash on upgrade would silently accept any edit, hence the legacy rule. Rejected: a shared `pgroup.sh` (breaks "no file sharing", and `cli-test` sources `discover.sh` alone); refusing `init` when no group method exists (a trial run is not worth a failed `init`, so that case keeps the direct kill); a line bound alone for the README (paragraphs shrank, lines did not, so bytes are bound too); a new skill or role for logging; a new skill or role for validation; imposing proofline's own log standard (the project writes its own in AGENTS.md); matching the whole file for the logging focus (an edit near an old log call would open it); an LLM or semantic detector and an `UNSUPPORTED` result for logging (reading the code needs no tool); refusing on a code-pattern match (uncertain, would block ordinary plans); one broad entry word list with refusal, and a removal exemption on any removal word (see Why); re-binding `risk_sha256` on upgrade (it would accept any edit); keeping a logging example as a comment in the AGENTS.md template and telling users to ignore it (the template is the hash-tracked block); a risk-signal focus for validation (it would produce findings from word matches). Limits, each decided: a log word match alone is never a finding, because only a path from a source to the log call is evidence; the logging focus sees only lines the diff adds, because matching old code would open it for unrelated edits; an entry point not listed in `## Contracts` is not seen, because PLAN.md is the only deterministic source before the freeze and code patterns are uncertain; the validation check proves that a line and a scenario exist, not that the limits are right or enforced, because that is the review's and the QA's work; a bullet that only mentions an entry word is information and never refuses, because a false refusal blocks an ordinary plan while the missed case is still listed by `pipeline` and the freeze note, and the corpus bounds the tiers; the validators carry the `validation.*` defaults but read the project's keys themselves, because they run without the CLI and must agree with the freeze; a run frozen before this rule keeps its focus set without `logging`, because upgrade must not invalidate a freeze; the discovery timeout copy of the driver's group functions is kept identical by a test, because the files share nothing; with no group method the discovery timeout kills only the direct child, because failing `init` for a trial run costs more than a possible leftover process; the shortcut tag list covers the tags in use and a new tag is added to the one variable.
- **A163. 0.0.21, the task file moves in the delivery commit, a missing CI check is said early, and `approve batch` starts the night driver in the background (amends A157, A160, A161).** What: (1) *Task file.* With the markdown adapter the commit step of `deliver` runs `agent.sh task-source-done <ID>`, which moves the task file to `done/` (Status line `done`) before `git add -A`, so the move is part of the delivery commit; the recorded task path and the completion-report receipt follow the file. Other adapters and a file already in `done/` are left alone. The merge-step text says that on a failed merge the move exists only in the unmerged pull request branch. (2) *No CI.* `agent.sh ci-check` prints `ci: present (<file>)`, `ci: absent` plus the warning "no CI check runs on pull requests here. proofline merge does not merge a PR that has no checks.", or `ci: unknown` (not a GitHub repository). `doctor` warns, the delivery summary warns, `batch-plan` writes a BLOCKER into the summary and refuses the batch, and `agent.sh ci-task-command` prints one ready `/proofline task Add a GitHub Actions workflow ...` line with the project's own build and test commands. (3) *Background driver.* After the user's "approve batch" the lead runs `agent.sh batch-start` (lead only): it starts the driver detached from the session (own process group, SIGHUP ignored, stdin `/dev/null`, output to `.agents/local/batch/driver.log`, `driver.pid` holds pid and group), under a sleep inhibitor (`caffeinate -i -w <pid>` on macOS, `systemd-inhibit` on Linux, plainly "none available" otherwise), refuses a second start, and prints `driver started, pid N, log <path>`, the inhibitor line and `plug in the computer; do not close the laptop lid.` (the batch summary says that line once too, and the summary hash binds it). `agent.sh batch-stop` and `proofline batch stop` stop only the driver they recorded (verified group, TERM, grace, KILL) and park the running task with the reason `stopped`; `batch status` starts with a `driver:` line. `proofline batch run` in a terminal stays the same driver. (4) *Next step.* `next-user.sh` reads the batch folder for `batch`, `batch status` and `batch stop` (states ci, nobatch, unapproved, running, approved, stopped, finished-parked, finished-clean): no CI gives the ready CI task line, not approved `reply "approve batch" in this session to start the batch`, started `driver started, log: .agents/local/batch/driver.log. In the morning: /proofline batch status`, a parked task `/proofline resume <ID>, then reply in this session to the open question: ...` (or `reopen`, `deliver`, `run` by the run's state), none parked the next queued task, stop `/proofline batch status`; `deliver` for a run that is ready, and `--error=ci`, give the CI task line. Why: the done-move happened after the merge, in a second commit nobody made, so the task stayed in `todo/`; a project with no CI discovered at the merge step that nothing could ever be delivered, and a batch repeated that failure once per task; the night command needed a terminal the user had to open and keep after approving in the session, which a closed session or a sleeping Mac broke. Rejected: a setting that lets `proofline merge` merge without CI (the merge rule stays: no checks, no merge); a separate terminal command after the approval (one more step to forget, and the session already knows the approval is valid); stopping by process name or pattern (it can hit another user's or project's process; the kill guard of A156 denies it, and the recorded group is exact); a watchdog that restarts the driver (a crash loop would repeat a half-done task; the user resumes it); moving the task file in a separate commit after the merge (a second approval and a dirty main). Not verified: the real `caffeinate` and `systemd-inhibit` were only exercised with fakes that honour `-w` and run the command, so whether the machine stays awake with the lid open, and what the lid does, is not proven here (closing the lid sleeps most laptops whatever the inhibitor holds, hence the reminder); the background start was tested on the process-group methods available on the test machines, not on every OS; the CI check reads workflow files for a `pull_request` trigger and does not know whether a branch rule requires the checks. Limits: `ci: unknown` is not a block; a stopped driver's running task is parked `stopped` and resumed by hand.
- **A164. 0.0.22, CI under ten minutes, a working agreement and test policy, and the product evidence model with the `product-spec` skill (amends A76; related A109, A153).** What: (1) *CI.* macOS jobs, the browser measurement (`visual-browser`) and the benchmark report run only on `schedule` and `workflow_dispatch`; pushes to main and pull requests are Linux only. (2) *Template.* `tests/shared/fixture-lib.sh` builds one installed template per content hash of the control-plane files, and `test-lib.sh` and `cli-test` copy it per scenario; each scenario still owns its copy; a changed control-plane file gives a new hash and a rebuild. (3) *Units.* `sc_every_state` and `cli-test` are scenario units. (4) *Weights.* CI uploads per-unit seconds (`SELFTEST_TIMES_FILE`); `tests/selftest-weights.sh` rebuilds the weights file; an unlisted unit counts as the median. (5) *Affected tests.* `tests/affected.sh` maps changed files to suites through `tests/affected-map.txt`; it decides only what can be skipped, and an unmapped, shared or uncertain change runs the suite. (6) *Working agreement.* CONTRIBUTING holds the lead, review and agent-hygiene rules and the test policy (agents run only `tests/affected.sh`; the full self-test is CI's job). (7) *Product.* Claims are fact, assumption or decision; a fact needs a source (web: URL, access date, quote; interview: id, quote; data: query or script and output hash); `product-spec` writes the PRD, checks it deterministically, gates splitting, checks requirement coverage and traces a requirement to its task, test, evidence and pull request from records; the reviewer role has the focus `spec`; intent-grill batches up to three lettered questions and asks for anti-goals. Why: a main push took 36 minutes because nine macOS jobs shared five free slots, every scenario installed proofline again, the weights were stale and two units took about 500 seconds each; product work needs the same evidence discipline as code. Rejected: a new role for PRD review (the reviewer role takes a focus); a catalogue of product frameworks (one lean PRD, detail on request); personas without a source; showing numeric scores as if measured; macOS on every main push (nine jobs, five slots, no extra signal over the nightly run).
- **A165. 0.0.23, product discovery and assessment (amends A164).** What: (1) *Discovery.* The `product-discovery` skill and `/proofline product discover frame|research|interviews|competitors|opportunities <slug>` write `FRAME.md`, `RESEARCH.md`, `INTERVIEWS.md`, `COMPETITORS.md` and `OPPORTUNITIES.md` in `<product.dir>/<slug>/`, each gated by a read-only checker. Claims have ids (`C-n`, `INT-n`, `OP-n`, `S-n`); a number that is not a sourced fact is `unsourced-number`; a competitor cell is value, URL, observation date and quote, and is flagged `stale` after `product.stale_days` (default 90); every quote must occur verbatim in the raw interview text, which lives only under `.agents/local/product-discovery/` and is never tracked. (2) *Reference.* The fact source `ref <slug>#C-n` lets a PRD cite a claim by id; it is valid only when the claim is itself a valid fact. (3) *Assessment.* The `product-assess` skill and `/proofline product assess assumptions|probes|premortem|redteam|decision <slug>` write `ASSUMPTIONS.md`, `PROBES.md`, `PREMORTEM.md`, `REDTEAM.md` and `DECISION.md`: importance and evidence are `high|medium|low`; a probe's thresholds are locked by a timestamp and a sha256 before the result, and a changed threshold is rejected, also against the committed version; the red team is the reviewer with focus `strategy` on an independent agent, at most 2 rounds; the decision is `build|clarify|stop` with ADR sections. (4) *Stop gate.* `check-prd.mjs --ready` refuses the split under an effective `decision: stop`. (5) *Outcome.* `/proofline product outcome <slug>` records metric id, baseline, script and output hashes and the value; `check-outcome.mjs` rejects a value that does not match the output file, and a lesson candidate needs approval (A149). (6) `/proofline trace` also shows the evidence, assumption, decision and outcome links, and every new command ends with the user's next step (A159). Why: a PRD built on numbers nobody can open, personas nobody met and thresholds chosen after the result only looks evidence-based; each link now has an id, a source or a hash a script can check. Rejected: a persona without a source (an invented person is an assumption and is written as one); raw interviews in git (personal data, and history cannot be unpublished); numeric scores presented as measured (a 1-10 risk score looks measured and is not; only high, medium and low are allowed); setting thresholds after the result (the probe is locked by hash first, so a result cannot move its own success line); a new reviewer role for the red team (the existing role with a focus is enough).

- **A166. 0.0.24, visual proof of native apps with the project's own tools; ui-design platform rules (amends A82, A147; related A98, A102).** What: (1) *Native measurement.* `visual-proof` gets `scripts/native.mjs` and `references/NATIVE.md`: `--detect` decides the target (Flutter, Android, iOS, React Native, desktop) from files and finds the project's own UI or screenshot test tool and run command (`--run` proves it once); uiautomator and generic JSON output are measured for labels, touch target size (44pt iOS, 48dp Android), large text, dark theme and a screenshot with sha256 per spec state; checks the output cannot show are listed `not_measured`; no tool or device is `UNSUPPORTED` with one suggestion line. The report validator accepts the native sections; web measurement is unchanged. (2) *ui-design.* `references/NATIVE.md` holds the native mobile rules (touch targets, platform navigation, system text size, dark theme, safe areas); UI.md gets an optional `target` (default `web`); `keyboard` per screen is required for web and optional for native mobile targets. Why: A82 limited visual proof to a browser, so a native app could only be reviewed by reading code; projects that already run Flutter, Espresso or XCUITest tests produce structured output that can be measured without proofline owning a device farm. Rejected: proofline installing its own mobile test tool or driving a simulator (heavy, platform-specific, and the project's command already produces the output); passing when no tool exists (A98: an unmeasured check is UNSUPPORTED, never PASS). Not tested: real emulator or device runs.
- **A167. 0.0.25 patch: batch driver tests, README headroom, native output formats documented (amends A157, A160, A161 for the batch test; A164 for the README bound note).** What: (1) *Batch test flakiness.* `sc_driver_stop` and `sc_driver_crash` in `control-plane/agents/scripts/batch-test.sh` failed now and then. Root cause: the scenarios' EXIT trap ran `kill "$unrel"` without `|| :`, and under `set -e` a failed kill (the helper process was already reaped, a race) exited the scenario with status 1 after its `ok` line. The driver was unaffected. Fix: the guard `|| :` in `batch-test.sh`. Verified with 64 runs of each scenario before (`sc_driver_stop` 4 failures, `sc_driver_crash` 1 failure) and 96 runs of each after, under 16 parallel workers, with 0 failures. (2) *README headroom.* The README is slimmed from 12482 to 10895 bytes; the bound is unchanged (12500 bytes, 178 lines). The "Real output" section moved to `docs/concepts/workflow.md`. (3) *Native output formats.* The formats `visual-proof` measures for native apps are documented in `docs/concepts/testing.md`; no new parser. Why: a test that exits 1 after its ok line hides real failures behind noise, and the README was within 18 bytes of its bound. Rejected: raising the README bound (the bound exists to keep the first page short); sleeping longer in the tests (hides the race and slows every run); writing parsers for more tools (A166 measures the project's own tool output and the documented formats are enough). Confidence: high.
- **A168. Release version bump script `tests/ci/bump-version.sh` (dev tooling; amends the Versions rules of CONTRIBUTING).** What: the 0.0.25 release commit replaced "0.0.24" everywhere it appeared and so rewrote three historical comments ("visual-proof for native apps (0.0.24)" in `native.mjs`, `validate-visual.mjs` and `visual-proof-native.test.mjs`) that say when a feature arrived; they are restored. `sh tests/ci/bump-version.sh <new-version>` now rewrites only the version-holding sites, each by an anchored pattern (`bin/proofline`, both plugin manifests, `config.yaml`, `playground/package.json`, `metadata.version` of every `SKILL.md`, the two `SKILL_VERSION` constants, `skills/proof-review/references/REPORTING.md`, the README status lines, the `adapters/README.md` heading, the two fixtures and the pinned expectations in `tests/cli/cli-test.sh` and four `tests/skills` files), moves the CHANGELOG `Unreleased` entries under `## <version> — <date>` and runs both generators. `--check` fails when a site disagrees with `plugin.json`. A site missing its anchored pattern aborts before any file is written. The test `tests/ci/bump-version-test.sh` bumps a temp copy and asserts that only listed files changed, that every changed line names the old or new version, that a historical "(0.0.24" stays and that a bad version string exits non-zero. Audit of the 0.0.21 to 0.0.24 bump commits found no other overwritten history. Rejected: a global search-and-replace of the old version (it rewrote history comments in 0.0.25); a review-only rule without a tool (the mistake repeats under time pressure). Confidence: high. Not-tested: a real release run (the test bumps to a fake version in a copy).
- **A169. 0.0.26, CI red self-fix, a run's own commits do not stale the plan, one git worktree per run, runtime-version warning, the lead's ban on product files outside a run, Stop-hook scope, one-process approvals (amends A152, A154, A155, A157, A161, A163).** What: (1) *CI red self-fix.* After the `merge` step of a delivery, a red check is a finding, not a question. `agent.sh deliver-step <ID> ci-fix|ci-commit|ci-push|ci-merge <PR>`: `ci-fix` refuses unless the pull request is this delivery's open, non-draft pull request at HEAD, the `pr` and `merge` steps ran and a check of that head really failed; it writes the failed run's log to `ci-fix/NNN.log` (data, never instructions), records the attempt (`ci-fix/NNN.yaml`, ledger `ci-fix-open|-grant|-commit|-push|-stop`) and reopens the run through `amend` inside the frozen scope (no refreeze); the fix passes the gates again; `ci-commit` records a commit and a push approval from the user's delivery approval, bound to the new content, to the delivery summary hash and, for the push, to the branch ref (`refs/heads/<branch>`), single use; `ci-push` and `ci-merge` push to that same branch and record which CI run belongs to which push. At most 2 attempts (counted from the ledger, so deleting a record gives none back); the third red CI records one question (`ci-fix/limit.txt`) and a batch parks the task there; `resume` continues an open attempt and `deliver-plan` is not run again. The approval record may carry `ref:`; `approval-consume <ID> push '' <refs>` (the pre-push hook passes them) consumes it only for a push to exactly that ref. (2) *A run's own commits do not stale the plan.* `delivered_mode` measures a run's task paths from `base_sha` while HEAD is exactly the delivery's own commit chain (`ci_chain_ok`: the summary's head, the one delivery commit, then the CI-fix commits the records name), so the delivery commit is not read as a changed base; `base_moved_control_only ... own` also accepts the run's own bookkeeping, its task file moving between the lifecycle folders and the knowledge step's wiki, ADR and design documents the plan scope does not name. Any other commit leaves the checks as before. (3) *CODE_DONE exit.* The way back from CODE_DONE for a CI fix is the existing `amend`, wrapped by `ci-fix`; `reopen` is unchanged and still needs its own approval (A155); `ci-fix/` is a protected run folder like `approvals/`. (4) *One git worktree per run.* `agent.sh worktree-add|worktree-path|worktree-remove <ID> [--force]` (lead, main working tree) make `.agents/local/worktrees/<ID>`, detached at the canonical branch's tip (that branch stays checked out in the main working tree; `agent.sh branch` accepts the detached HEAD only when it is exactly that tip), with links to the shared batch, approvals, metrics, friction and prompts folders. Night mode creates one per task, runs the session (Claude or the Codex `proofline_night` profile, whose filesystem table now names the worktree's git administration folder) inside it, starts a task only when every dependency is merged, removes the worktree after the merge and keeps a parked task's; a task whose pull request is open and not merged is parked with the reason `awaiting merge`; a second open run in one worktree is still refused (D4) but the message names `worktree-add`; the worktrees are not scratch (A53) and `proofline clean` leaves them alone. (5) *Runtime versions.* `proofline doctor` and the verifier run the read-only `skills/verify-proof/scripts/runtime-versions.sh`, which compares node, dotnet, go and python as installed with what the project's CI specifies (workflow `*-version`, `.nvmrc`, `package.json` engines, `global.json`, `go.mod`, `.tool-versions`, ...); `doctor` prints WARN on a different major (major.minor for go and python), never FAIL; VERIFY.md gets a `## Runtime` section with the script's lines, `validate-verify.mjs` checks the format, and a mismatch never changes the verdict. (6) *The lead writes no product file outside a run.* With no run active the PreToolUse hook denies the main session every write except task files (`tasks.dir`), `.agents/`, the knowledge scope and design documents, git-ignored files and paths outside the project; the denial says to propose a light run with `/proofline run <text>`; the shell scanner judges shell writes (a heuristic) and `proofline doctor` probes both the denial and the task-file pass. (7) *Stop hook scope.* The identifier check (A154) runs only for the final report of a run, the last message of a turn in which the lead ran publish-completion-report, cleanup, terminate, a deliver command or batch-status/batch-check; an ordinary answer is never checked. (8) *One-process approvals.* `agent.sh approve <ID> <KIND>...` takes several kinds in one process (locks and content fingerprint once, each kind recorded as a single `approve` would; a refused kind does not stop the next; none recorded fails), and `approve-pending` makes one call per target with the delivery steps in the order commit, push, pr, merge, cleanup, so the 30 s time limit of a Claude Code UserPromptSubmit hook cannot cut five processes off after the fourth. If a cut-off still leaves a prefix, `deliver-step` stops with a text saying that the reply was recorded only in part, that nothing was run, that the summary is still valid and that the user must send `approve delivery` once more. Why: a red CI used to stop the delivery with a question for a failure the gates could fix; the run's own delivery commit made its plan look stale; one run per worktree blocked every other task behind a parked one and dirtied the main tree; a green local run in another language version proves little; a lead that edits files with no run active defeats the ban on product code (D2); the Stop hook sent the lead back on plain answers; and in a real project the approval hook was cut off before the last step. Rejected: raising the hook time limit (the limit belongs to the runtime and a longer one only moves the cut-off; the fix is less work per call); asking the user at the first red CI (the error is in the log and the gates already bound the fix); an unlimited number of CI-fix rounds (an unbounded loop that pushes without a person); a push approval that covers any ref (a CI fix must not reach another branch); making a runtime mismatch a FAIL (the versions are advice, a different runtime may be intended); one shared working tree with better locking (the open-run, ACTIVE_RUN and hooks state is per worktree); checking every Stop message for identifiers (ordinary answers are not reports). Confidence: medium. Not-tested: a real CI-fix round against a hosted CI (the tests use a fake `gh`), a real Codex session in a task worktree, an approval hook cut off by a real runtime.

- **A170. 0.0.27, design craft: visitor modes, patterns as defaults, a measured design group, source lint and an image ledger (rebalances the 0.0.4 `avoided` rule of A146; amends A81, A82, A158, A166).** What: (1) *Visitor mode.* `ui-design` gives every surface a mode, `persuade`, `operate`, `read` or `experience`, chosen by the surface (a tool's landing page is persuade) and written into the UI spec with a reason; boldness follows the mode, full courage and imagery for persuade and experience, precision and restraint for operate and read. (2) *Idea before design.* Persuade and experience surfaces get a topic inventory of 8-12 things that belong to this product, an argument (one claim and its proof per section, each proof a product fact or a path in the code), and an idea sentence that must stop being true when the nearest rival's name is put into it; three directions dealt by `scripts/deal.mjs` from the bundled library with a recorded seed (the designer derives no candidates, see (9) and (10)) plus the calm category-standard option for the user to choose, and one live interaction with a no-script fallback. (3) *Patterns are defaults.* A dated registry of 15 pattern ids (`Registry date: 2026-10-23`) replaces a ban list: using one needs a line `- <id>: <reason>` under `## Pattern justifications` in `DESIGN.md`; when an ornament goes, fullness, real imagery, motion and craft go in (fullness rule, visual plan, typography, colour, motion thesis, browser surfaces, copy and controls, operate rules, each in a reference file loaded on demand). The 0.0.4 rule `avoided` (A146) is rebalanced, not repealed: `avoided` may still name ids, but absence of ornament is no longer the goal and an empty page is a defect. (4) *Validator.* `validate-ui.mjs` requires the mode, the argument, the inventory, idea, dealt directions (re-dealt from the seed), live thing, visual plan, motion and font plan where the mode demands them, rejects file paths and internal ids as UI copy, headlines over 48 characters and registry misuse; existing fixtures got the new fields, no expected file was loosened. (5) *Design group.* `visual-proof` gets an optional `design` block of `visual.json` and `report.json`, modelled on `seo` (A158): fixed viewports 1440, 768 and 390, checks for heading lines, type scale, body measure, loaded fonts, edge margins, edge text, vacuum, untitled sections, empty hero, theme surfaces, untranslated text, motion count, reduced motion, text without script, pinned scenes photographed at 0, 50 and 100 percent, and the image ledger; a failed item blocks, an item that cannot be measured is `not_measured` with a reason. (6) *Lint and ledger.* `scripts/lint-source.mjs` scans project source for the registry patterns it can detect honestly and writes `lint.json`; a finding is blocking unless `DESIGN.md` justifies its id; `validate-visual.mjs` counts blocking findings as FAIL reasons. `docs/design/IMAGES.md` lists every raster image and self-hosted font with licence and credit or source. (7) *Critique.* COMPLEX and CRITICAL reports with a design group carry 14 judged items, each `ok` or `concern` with a reason and a screenshot; a numeric score is rejected and the critique never closes the gate. (8) *Flow.* The implementer of a UI task runs the auditor, looks at the screenshots with the critique, fixes the three most important problems and repeats at most three rounds (a self-check, never a substitute for QA); for persuade and experience the designer writes `DESIGN.md` first as the direction contract and the knowledge step updates it from the world as built; under `conform` the existing system is unchanged and a redesign needs approval. Why: a site built under 0.0.26 felt like a years-old beginner site (hairline boxes, monospace labels showing file paths, numbered steps, one neon accent on a dark ground, an empty hero, no imagery or motion, untranslated English on a Turkish page); the 0.0.4 `avoided` rule had removed ornaments and put nothing in their place, and nothing measured the result. Rejected: catalogues of looks dressed onto a product (the library below is decisions, picked by a deterministic script, and the idea, rival test and argument steps still force adaptation); an external detector binary (the checks are our own scripts, nothing is installed); a numeric design score (a score invites tuning to the number; the critique is reasoned ok or concern); making image generation mandatory (the visual plan states what is real, and none is allowed). Addendum, same release: (9) *Design library, reversing the 0.0.4/A146 decision of no ready-made style catalogue on purpose.* `skills/ui-design/library/` holds data files from two MIT-licensed sources recorded in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` (the only file that names them, with pinned commits, sha256 per included file and the full licence texts; the static name check excludes exactly that file, the one exception to the third-party-name rule). The included files (directions, heroes, sections, type systems, palettes, motions, app styles, photo sets, the UX guideline list, chart guide, app-interface list and the font-licence directory) are byte-identical copies; our own adaptation layer lives apart in `library/adapt/`, never as edits to the copies; the sources' method documents and their style, palette and colour catalogues are not copied. `tests/ci/sync-design-library.sh` re-fetches at the recorded commits (`--update <source-id> <commit>` moves a pin) and `--check` verifies the copies are byte-identical (tests use a local mirror, no network). Reason for the reversal: the library is not a theme dressed onto a product but composition, typography and motion decisions extracted from real sites, while candidates a model derives from its own habits fall into the same moulds; the idea sentence, the rival test and the argument still force adaptation to the product. (10) *Deal.* `scripts/deal.mjs` replaces designer-derived candidates: inputs are surface mode, sector, tone, the imagery that really exists, section roles and brand constants; it picks three directions with hero, section templates, type system, palette and motion (operate: app style, shell, pattern per screen), never one that needs absent imagery, calls no model and no network, and the same seed gives the same result. A single slot is redrawn only with a written reason (`--redraw <slot> --reason`); content that does not fit a template changes the template, not the content; subject colours replace dealt colours when they clash and the ratios stay. The calm standard option stays beside the three and the designer never recommends it; `ui.json` records inputs, seed, dealt ids and redraws and the validator replays the deal. (11) *Fonts and images.* The library names faces, the implementer self-hosts them and verifies the licence from the font-licence directory or the package's own licence file, writing it to the ledger `## Fonts`; a font whose licence cannot be verified is not used and deal offers a same-voice system that is verifiable. Library photos are used only from sets licensed for commercial use, downloaded and self-hosted, address verified, credit kept on the page and in the ledger; they are never the project's own imagery and never count as deal's existing imagery. (12) *UX rules in QA.* The UX guideline list with its severities becomes a checklist: measurable high-severity rules are measured by the auditor as `design_ux_*` checks, the rest are judged (`ux_review`); `visual-proof` stays installable alone through its own derived `skills/visual-proof/ux-rules.json` (rule id, severity, `measure`, our own short wording) and a test checks every id against the library when both skills are present; the chart guide chooses the chart type for surfaces with charts. (13) *Focus modes.* `/proofline design ui <mode> <target>` with `bolder quieter polish animate typeset colorize layout delight harden adapt` runs like any TRIVIAL or STANDARD task, touches only the target, keeps the system, adds no colour, font or element unless asked, and `scripts/scope-check.mjs` verifies afterwards that nothing outside the target changed. (14) *Comp and model.* When the runtime has an image-generation tool a persuade surface gets a draft visual of the chosen direction before coding, the approved comp is the implementer's target and QA's reference, the skip is written when there is no tool, and generated images enter the ledger; UI tasks with a persuade or experience surface run designer and implementer on the strongest model (signal `ui_persuade`). (15) *Reality check.* Deal was run (seed 7, no model, no network) for three briefs: a restaurant's QR menu site and admin panel fit (three warm photo-led directions with verified fonts; three commerce-style app styles), a developer tool's marketing site and docs fit well (real-UI fragment heroes, quiet and technical directions, none needing photographs), and a city-tour map app was partial: the library has no map-canvas screen pattern, so deal reports none for the map screen and the designer draws it from shell and tokens, while its landing page fits (colour-field and type-poster heroes, no photograph-led template; library photo sets offered self-hosted and credited). (16) *Deliberate deviations and hardening.* `design_body_measure` at the phone390 viewport accepts 30-75 characters (a 390 px column cannot hold 45 at a readable size) while 1440 and 768 keep 45-75; a requested design check that ends `not_measured` makes the visual result INCONCLUSIVE (submitted as UNSUPPORTED with a prefixed reason, like core cells), never PASS; every design item's status is validated against its value and threshold, motion_min included; deal flags `photo_dependent` directions (judged from the direction's own text as well as its section tags) and deals one only when nothing else satisfies the brief's imagery, offering the usable library photo sets; the idea sentence must contain the rival's name (`idea.rival`); `scope-check.mjs` uses no rename detection (`--no-renames`), so a rename counts as a delete plus an add; the static name check ignores case, hyphens and spaces and skips only the notices file and the byte-identical copies it lists; library data copies keep the sources' own strings, as the licence requires, which is why those copies are the only other place such text may appear. Rejected, also: hot-linking library photos or fonts (a CDN outage or licence change would break the site; self-hosting is verifiable); copying the sources' method documents (ui-design stays in one voice); taking their style and palette catalogues; a model call inside deal (the pick must be reproducible from the seed). Confidence: medium. Not-tested: real browsers on every viewport in CI (the browser tests skip where none exists), the critique's judgement quality across models, a full persuade-site run end to end.

- **A171. 0.0.28, retro findings of a COMPLEX run (65 scope paths, 7 failed gates, one FAILED run) (amends A76, A148, A157, A159, A169; related A136, A154, A156).** What: 18 findings, in six groups. (A) *Findings and rounds.* (1) A blocking `rules` or `rules+tests` row must carry `scan: <command>` and `instances: single` or the full `path:line` list, or `record` rejects it; `roles/reviewer.md` says so, and script-added rows (evidence starting with `agent.sh`, a prefix `submit` refuses in a reviewer's own rows) are exempt, and a scan must be at least two words and ten characters. A failing record costs no fix round when every blocking row cites a line that already existed in the previously reviewed revision and was not cited before (`gate_carryover_free`, compared by the two fingerprint trees); a re-cited line, a new or changed line (the cited line is mapped through the diff of the two trees and must be unchanged at its position, so a new copy of an old line counts), or a missing earlier tree counts as a round. (2) `GATES.md` has a written "Blocking or info" rule with a version; every gate record stores `blocking_rule`, and `record` rejects an observation that flips between info and blocking while the rule version is unchanged (matched by the cited line's text, or by normalised finding text when the line does not resolve). (3) A mutation budget overrun (21/20) is a `WARNING` line from the validator, printed by `submit`, and no longer blocks. (B) *Gate order and build record.* (4) Gate dependencies are written in `GATES.md` and enforced in `next`, `decide` and `record`: VERIFY needs the QA `visual` focus when the run has one; `pipeline` prints `gate.VERIFY.needs=QA:visual`. (5) `handoff IMPLEMENTED` writes the build record (when build or test is configured; a failing build never blocks the handoff); a missing record is the first step `next` lists. (C) *Knowledge.* (6) Drift in knowledge-scope documents (`DESIGN.md`, `design_docs`) is `info` at the verifier's submit; `knowledge-done` and delivery refuse while design-sync still shows it, when the run has `design/SYNC.md`; no waiver or wider scope is needed. (7) The stale gates after the knowledge step reproduced. Gates, result headers, waivers and `next` now bind `gate_fingerprint`, the content fingerprint without documentation files (`.md`, `.json`, `.yaml`, `.yml`, `.txt`) under `knowledge_scope_root` and `design_docs` paths (unless a plan scope names the path; an executable or any other file there stays counted, so `docs/design/x.sh` binds the gates); approvals, CI and delivery summaries keep the full tree, so the user still approves every byte. A run in flight at upgrade sees its gates stale once. (D) *Approvals and answers.* (8) `approve-pending` writes its output to `.agents/local/approve.log`, shows an unrecorded approval as one line with the reason to the lead and the user, cleans `.approving-` markers older than 2 minutes on the next prompt, and `agent.sh next` records the approval from the user's newest message when the hook could not. (9) Every hook run is timed; the hook spools `name|ms|result` to `.agents/local/hook-times` without a lock and `agent.sh hook-time` moves it into the ledger as one aggregated `hook` event per name plus a `hook-timeout` event per timeout; the friction report counts them. The prompt hook records the capture first, then runs the approval step with a 20 s budget and an 8 s lock wait (`PROOFLINE_HOOK_APPROVE_BUDGET`, `PROOFLINE_HOOK_LOCK_WAIT`); a hook the runtime cut off leaves an in-flight marker that the next hook records as a timeout. Measured on a copy of a real project: 0.4 to 0.8 s per prompt hook; the 31 s timeouts came from `approve` waiting up to 120 s for the lock. (10) `answer --captured` takes the newest user message after the question and prints which one (`source_message:` in the answer file). (11) `answer --amend` replaces a recorded answer with a newer, unused user message; the old record is kept as `NNN.answer.amended-K.md` with `amended: true`; stdin is ignored, so the lead alone cannot amend. (12) Run files stay English; the user's own words (answers, approval replies) are stored in a quote block, every line starting with `> `, which the language check exempts in the script-written answer and approval files and in TASK.md, and in any other run file only when the quoted or fenced line repeats a captured user prompt (the 3-letter threshold counts per file in every whole-file scan, at freeze, at submit and at the handoff to DONE; the write-time check sees only the text being written, so a file built from several small edits is caught by those scans, and a per-file edit counter was rejected as state that the scans make redundant); the check runs at write time (`agent.sh language-check` from the write hook for Write, Edit and MultiEdit under `.agents/runs/`), not at delivery. (E) *Size and time.* (13) Freeze stops when the scope has more paths than `pipelines.<CLASS>.max_scope_paths` (TRIVIAL 2, STANDARD 15, COMPLEX 15, CRITICAL 12) and proposes a split; a user answer starting with "proceed without splitting" (no negation or condition), bound to that exact scope list, is recorded as a `split_override` ledger event and lets it continue. (14) The dispatch timeout comes from `dispatch_timeout_seconds` per class (900, 1800, 3600, 3600); the in-flight marker records its own timeout; a timeout writes `dispatch/RESUME-<role>.md` (log tail, changed paths, run artifacts) that the next dispatch of the role carries in its prompt. (F) *Hook and lead.* (15) The hook reads a variable that the same command assigns a plain literal (`ID=T-6; sh agent.sh next $ID`) in argument position, checks the literal like any word (so `c=rec; d=ord; $c$d` is denied as `record`), and allows reading exactly `/tmp/claude-<uid>/<project>/<session>/tasks/<file>.output` as the operand of cat, head, tail, grep, wc or `sed -n` (never run, sourced or given to a shell); `printf -v`, `let` and any `IFS` assignment also drop the tracked variables; variables from command substitution, `read`, loops or conditional assignment, a variable as the command word or script path, writes to the task files and `..` stay denied; denials show the allowed form in one line. (16) A bare `agent.sh decide` is refused (`dispatch.sh` writes a one-use nonce file under `.agents/local/dispatch/` for its own `decide` call and passes the nonce as `PROOFLINE_DISPATCH`, which `agent.sh` accepts only while that file exists; the hook also denies any command that names the variable, as written or after removing quotes and variable references, or the nonce folder); `decide --dry` reads without writing a file or ledger row; `decide --agent-path` records the decision of the Agent route, once per role and focus until a result of that role is recorded; the output starts with `decision: role= runtime= model= effort= reason=`. (17) `agent.sh friction-note <ID> user-command|user-shell` records that the user was asked to run a command, a proofline bug; the friction report counts it and the Stop hook notes such a message when it asks the user to run the command ("please run", "run:", an imperative line, or a fence introduced by one), not when it merely mentions it; a prompt hook stopped at its budget also stops the children of the approval step, by pid. (18) The lead's wait messages are one line (`agent.sh wait-line <ID> [WHAT] [--done]`: `waiting: <what>, <M>m<SS>s so far`). Also fixed: the friction report printed `n/a`, which its own character check rejects, and now says `unknown`; the shared language rule in `tests/shared/shared-rules.md` and every skill carries the quote-block wording. Why: the run's friction report showed VERIFY failing 3 times and REVIEW_RULES 4 times, 7 gate failures, 4 asks, 3 fix rounds and a dispatch timeout; the lead and the user did work that scripts can do. The evidence folder of the run was cleaned before the fix, so each item is shown from the code and the friction report. Rejected: matching the "same class" of a finding by its text (too fuzzy to be deterministic); making the whole next failing record free (an unfixed line could loop); re-binding gate records after the knowledge step (it weakens the record; excluding the files is simpler, with approvals still bound to the whole tree); expanding variables used as command words (an obfuscation vector); trusting any variable; a hook-only decide check (the script is the foundation); a second quote syntax; a CLI flag for the split override (not user-backed); a per-call ledger line for hook timings; telling the user to run `approve` in a terminal; keeping "oldest unused message" for `--captured`; editing the old answer file; blocking the Stop hook (A154 chose no rewrite loop). Confidence: medium. Not-tested: a real runtime kill of the prompt hook (simulated with a dead in-flight marker), the full self-test under load (CI runs it).
